In this case, we have
F_{0}(3)=3
, but
\{\beta\in\Gamma_{1}\mid t(\beta)=3\}=\{\beta\}
whereas
\{\alpha\in Q_{1}\mid t(\alpha)=3)\}=\{\beta,\gamma\}
, in particular
F
is not a covering. But, clearly
F
is an winding.

The following is proven in
[
Fra21
, Lemma 3]
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
Q
be a quiver.

(1)

Let
c:\Gamma\to Q
be a universal cover and
d:D\to Q
be any cover. Then, there exists a quiver map
p:\Gamma\to D
such that
c=d\circ p
.

(2)

If
Q
is connected then
Q
admits a universal cover.

The following proposition characterizes quivers whose coefficients quivers have a non-degenerate nice grading.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
Q
be a quiver.
Q
has no loop at a vertex if and only if any winding
c:\Gamma\to Q
has a non-degenerate nice grading.

Proof.

(\Leftarrow):
We first note
Q
has no loop at a vertex if and only if
c:Q^{\prime}\hookrightarrow Q
has a non-degenerate nice grading for all subquivers
Q^{\prime}
of
Q
. Now, the first statement is clear as any inclusion
c:Q^{\prime}\hookrightarrow Q
is a winding.

(\Rightarrow):
For a given winding
c:\Gamma\to Q
, we can directly define a nice grading
\partial
on
\Gamma
as follows. We first label vertices of
Q
as
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
. For each vertex
w\in\Gamma_{0}
, we let
\partial(w)=i
of and only if
c(w)=v_{i}
. Since
Q
does not have a loop at any vertex,
\partial:\Gamma_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}
is a non-degenerate nice grading.
∎

For any quiver
Q
, one can construct a winding
c:\Gamma\to Q
which has a nice grading distinguishing vertices. This construction depends on a choice of an arrow of
Q
, and hence creates a family of windings distinguishing vertices. Note that this construction is a covering of
Q
, and any subquiver of
\Gamma
has a nice grading distinguishing vertices.

Construction 5.6
.

Let
Q
be a quiver. Let’s fix an arrow
e\in Q_{1}
and we label vertices of
Q
as
1,\dots,|Q_{0}|
. Let
\tilde{Q}_{e}
be a quiver obtained from
Q
by removing
e
while keeping all vertices. Let
\ell\in\{1,\dots,|Q_{0}|-1\}
. Let
\Gamma_{e}
be a quiver defined as follows:

(35)

(\Gamma_{e})_{0}:=\bigsqcup_{1\leq k\leq\ell}(\tilde{Q}_{e,k})_{0},

where each
\tilde{Q}_{e,k}=\tilde{Q}_{e}
. For the arrows
(\Gamma_{e})_{1}
, we keep all arrows of
\tilde{Q}_{e,k}
, and for each pair
(k,k+1)
, we add an arrow from a vertex
s(e)
of
\tilde{Q}_{e,k}
to
t(e)
of
\tilde{Q}_{e,k+1}
, where we use the same labeling as
Q
.

where the red arrows are newly added for
\Gamma_{e}
. Now, there is a natural winding
c:\Gamma_{e}\to Q
. Namely, we send
\tilde{Q}_{e,k}
to
Q
“identically”, and send the red arrows to
e
.

One can easily see, as in Example
5.7
, that there is a winding
c:\Gamma_{e}\to Q
for any quiver
Q
, where
\Gamma_{e}
is as in (
35
). In fact, the following is straightforward from the definition.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
Q
be a quiver, and
c:\Gamma_{e}\to q
be a natural winding as explained above. Then
\Gamma_{e}
is a covering of
Q
.

Our next step is to equip
\Gamma_{e}
is a nice grading distinguishing vertices as follows:

(1)

Let
L
be labeling of the vertices of
Q
. We assign an integer
m(\alpha)
to each arrow
\alpha\in Q_{1}
as
L(t(\alpha))-L(t(\alpha))
except
e
. We assign a “large enough number” to
e
so that we can assign different integers to the vertices of the copies of
\tilde{Q}_{e}
(see Example
5.9
).

(2)

Now, we define
\partial:(\Gamma_{e})_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}
. For
\tilde{Q}_{e,1}
, we use
L
, i.e., for each
u\in(\tilde{Q}_{e,1})_{0}
, we define
\partial(u)=L(u)
. For
\tilde{Q}_{e,2}
, we let
\partial(t(e))=\partial(s(e))+m(e)
. Then, we define
\partial
for the remaining vertices of
\tilde{Q}_{e,2}
by using
m(\alpha)
so that
m(\alpha)=\partial(t(\alpha))-\partial(s(\alpha))
.

(3)

Then, one iterate this process to define
\partial:(\Gamma_{e})_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}
.

Example 5.9
.

The following is the case when
e=9
from Example
5.7
.

\Gamma=\hbox to318.5pt{\vbox to77.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 159.24994pt\lower-38.64587pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-159.24994pt}{-34.98615pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}

From the above, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
Q
be a quiver,
e\in Q_{1}
, and
\Gamma_{e}
be as in (
35
). Then, the natural winding map
c:\Gamma_{e}\to Q
has a nice grading which distinguishes vertices.

Remark 5.11
.

In fact, one could iterate Construction
5.6
as much as one wants. Also, one could remove more edges (rather than just one edge) to do the same construction under which Proposition
5.10
still holds.

5.2.
Contraction of coefficient quivers

Next, we investigate how contractions and restrictions play a role in constructing a nice sequence distinguishing vertices. Let
Q
be a quiver, and
c:\Gamma\to Q
a winding. Let
A\subseteq Q_{1}
. Then, we have an induced winding
c|_{A}:\Gamma[c^{-1}(A)]\to Q
, where
\Gamma[c^{-1}(A)]
is the induced subgraph on
c^{-1}(A)
. Also we have the following map:

(36)

c/A:\Gamma/c^{-1}(A)\to Q/A.

The following example shows that (
36
) does not have to be a winding in general.

Example 5.12
.

which is not an winding.

Let
Q
be a quiver, and
c:\Gamma\to Q
a winding. For a nice grading
\partial:\Gamma_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}
, one can obtain a function
\hat{\partial}:\Gamma_{1}\to\mathbb{Z}
defined by
\hat{\partial}(\alpha)=\partial(t(\alpha))-\partial(s(\alpha))
for
\alpha\in\Gamma_{1}
. The function
\hat{\partial}
satisfies the condition (
11
). Conversely, for a function
\hat{\partial}:\Gamma_{1}\to\mathbb{Z}
satisfying the condition (
11
), one can find a corresponding nice grading
\partial
up to addition of a constant. More generally, for a given nice sequence
\partial=\{\partial_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, we obtain a sequence of functions
\{\hat{\partial}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, where
\hat{\partial}_{i}:\Gamma_{1}\to\mathbb{Z}
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and they satisfies the condition
(3)
in Definition
2.21
. Conversely, a sequence of functions
\{\hat{\partial}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
recovers
\partial=\{\partial_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
up to addition of constants.

Definition 5.13
.

With the same notation as above, we say that
\partial=\{\partial_{0},\dots,\partial_{n}\}
frees
\alpha\in\Gamma_{1}
if we can define a
\{\partial_{0},\dots,\partial_{n}\}
-nice grading
\partial_{n+1}
such that there is no
\beta\in\Gamma_{1}
such that
c(\alpha)=c(\beta)
,
\partial_{i}(s(\alpha))=\partial_{i}(s(\beta))
, and
\partial_{i}(t(\alpha))=\partial_{i}(t(\beta))
for
i=0,\dots,n+1
.

Example 5.14
.

Let
Q=\mathbb{L}_{2}
, with arrow set
Q_{1}=\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}
. Let
M
be the representation with quiver

In this case, any nice grading frees
\alpha_{2}
.

Remark 5.15
.

Let
c:\Gamma\to Q
be a winding, and
\partial=\{\partial_{0},\dots,\partial_{n}\}
be a nice sequence distinguishing vertices. Then,
\partial
frees all arrows in
\Gamma_{1}
since the condition (
12
) cannot happen in this case. Conversely, if a nice sequence
\partial=\{\partial_{0},\dots,\partial_{n}\}
frees all arrows in
\Gamma_{1}
, then
\partial
distinguishes vertices.

Proposition 5.16
.

Let
Q
be a quiver, and
c:\Gamma\to Q
a winding. Let
A\subseteq Q_{1}
such that

c/A:\Gamma/c^{-1}(A)\to Q/A\quad\textrm{and}\quad c|_{A}:\Gamma[c^{-1}(A)]\to Q

are windings which have a nice sequence distinguishing vertices. Then,
\Gamma
has a nice sequence distinguishing vertices.

Proof.

Let
\partial=\{\partial_{0},\dots,\partial_{k}\}
be a nice sequence distinguishing vertices for
c/A:\Gamma/c^{-1}(A)\to Q/A
. Each
\partial_{i}
can be lifted to define a grading
\hat{\partial}_{i}:\Gamma_{1}\to\mathbb{Z}
by setting
\hat{\partial}_{i}(\alpha)=0
for any
\alpha\in c^{-1}(A)
. Let
\partial_{i}^{\prime}:\Gamma_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}
be a grading obtained by
\hat{\partial}_{i}
. In this case, one can easily check that the lifted sequence of gradings
\partial^{\prime}=\{\partial_{0}^{\prime},\dots,\partial_{k}^{\prime}\}
is a nice sequence which frees the arrows of
\Gamma
which are not in
c^{-1}(A)
.

Now, we restrict
\Gamma
to
\Gamma[c^{-1}(A)]
. From the assumption, we obtain a nice sequence distinguishing vertices
\{\partial_{k+1},\dots,\partial_{n}\}
. Again, this sequence can be lifted to define a nice sequence
\partial^{\prime\prime}=\{\partial_{k+1}^{\prime},\dots,\partial_{n}^{\prime}\}
which frees the arrows in
c^{-1}(A)
. In particular, by adding one more grading if needed, we may assume that
\partial_{n}(u)\neq\partial_{n}(v)
for any vertices
u
of
\Gamma/c^{-1}(A)
and
v\in\Gamma[c^{-1}(A)]
. Now, one can easily check that
(\partial_{0}^{\prime},\dots,\partial_{n}^{\prime})
is a nice sequence distinguishing vertices.
∎

If
c:\Gamma\to Q
,
c/A:\Gamma/c^{-1}(A)\to Q/A
, and
c|_{A}:\Gamma[c^{-1}(A)]\to Q
are windings and
\Gamma
has a nice sequence distinguishing vertices, then does
c/A
also have a nice sequence distinguishing vertices? The following example shows that it is not the case.

Example 5.17
.

Q
and
\Gamma
.

Let
c:\Gamma\to Q
be an winding sending each arrow of
\Gamma
to the corresponding arrow in
Q
. From
[
JS21
, Lemma 5.18]
,
c
has a nice sequence distinguishing vertices. Let
A=\{7,8,9\}
. Clearly,
c|_{A}
is a winding with a nice sequence distinguishing vertices. However, one can easily check that
c/A
is a winding which does not have a nice sequence distinguishing vertices (again from
[
JS21
, Lemma 5.18]
).
